rights, is a fundamental human right. it is possible if we tackle the real causes, which for us are inseparable from the neoliberal drift which is reflected in the fundamental trends, particularly in the macroeconomic field, and which are evidenced by the priority given to the stability pact and the nominal convergence criteria and, therefore, by the fact that social cohesion is relegated to the background, this being generally accompanied by a complete deregulation of labour, the encouragement of privatisations, mergers and relocations, which usually generate unemployment, but also by a profoundly restrictive budgetary policy at community and national level. so we are now witnessing a clear consolidation of the foundations of the 'thatcher's' approach, sweetened, it is true, by aid measures. in the past, it was based on openly conservative principles, and today it is based on the argument of a so-called global pragmatism and on the name of a deified competitiveness. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one. it is essential to abandon the monetarist and anti-social perspective in order to place the human person permanently at the centre of priorities and policies. my group, which attaches particular importance to social issues, met last week in lisbon for the next extraordinary council to analyse and debate this issue. on this occasion we heard and debated with representatives of workers, portuguese trade unionists and others, including representatives of abb and michelin, as well as the promoters of the march for jobs. we have drawn conclusions summarised in 14 points which we will formally communicate to the council presidency. we believe that the main thing is to adopt macroeconomic policy measures focused on boosting research and public investment and on respecting the renewed public services, geared towards full employment and guaranteeing a level of employment of around 75% in 2010. it is necessary to ensure a more favourable distribution of income to labour, to ensure the taxation of speculative capital movements and to combat tax fraud and evasion, to curb mergers and relocations, especially when they are aimed at and accompanied by redundancies, to continue along the path of reducing working time without reducing wages and without degrading the quality of employment, to reconsider the union's financial perspective, to give decisive support to small and mediumsized enterprises, to place a strong emphasis on a renewed education system and on more and better vocational training, and to put an end to discrimination, the victims of which are mainly women and young people. it is in this light and in the light of the ability to define concrete, properly quantified and planned objectives in this vital area that we will analyse and judge the results of the lisbon summit.
- (pt) madam president, ladies and gentlemen, mr president-in-office of the council and prime minister of portugal, we are all as attentive as we have been to employment issues, and we must take note of the portuguese presidency's initiative to put the future of the social model back on the european policy agenda. however, before i comment on this subject, i would like to draw attention to certain aspects of the path traveled by europe in the last ten years, in order to put the next ten years in perspective in a realistic way, as proposed to us by the portuguese presidency. more or less ten years ago, when we were discussing the single currency and nominal convergence, some people in the decision-making and debate insisted on the need to give priority to employment, real convergence of economies and economic and social cohesion. many of us then expressed doubts and proposed alternatives to the course that was set, so that the challenges we face today would be addressed in a timely manner. however, the supporters of the single currency have focused their attention almost exclusively on its adoption, thinking that with it, with the euro, the social and development problems of the european union and in particular those of some economically weaker member states would be solved. as time went on and the single currency, by itself, failed to create jobs, heads of state and government were moved to action. meeting in luxembourg, they decided to create guidelines for member states to develop their own national employment plans, which they were to submit to the community bodies. the plans were still being discussed, and there was already a call for a new summit to create an employment pact, which was done in cologne, without having time to consider the practical consequences of what had been decided in luxembourg. the cardiff process was adopted in the wake of this, in other words we have come a long way, sometimes without much articulation, which we must recall in order to put the necessary changes in course into perspective. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have read carefully the document prepared by the portuguese presidency for the lisbon summit, which goes far beyond employment issues and which speaks of an information society and social cohesion. we are not, of course, discussing the strategic objective of making the european union a more dynamic and globally competitive economic area based on innovation and knowledge, with higher levels of economic growth, more and better jobs, and greater social cohesion. but let no one tell us that this strategic objective is new, for it is not. it is rather surprising how passive many governments in the european union have been in their support of the us experiment in using new technologies as a factor for economic growth and job creation, and how little has been done to support, above all, the multitude of small and mediumsized enterprises which have had difficulty adapting to the new reality and benefiting from information technology. i would also like to make it clear that we defend the european social model in contrast to certain social models in other parts of the world, but we defend it on the assumption that it is a model that must be improved and implemented in a shared way. we do not see the lisbon summit as another meeting where grand words are spoken, but simply as irresponsible proclamations. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, as long as there are 400% of disparities between minimum wage levels in the union, the real europe will not be complete. as long as there are double-digit unemployment rates, or genuine pockets of hidden unemployment within the european union, the real europe will not be completed. until families are generally recognised as the essential nucleus of human and social development, the real europe will not be complete. until the increasing number of poor and excluded people in the union are given priority attention by public authorities and society, the real europe will not be completed. until we tackle the demographic problem in the european union head-on, which will lead to the crisis of social systems and the existence of a fourth age, without paying particular attention to it, the real europe will not be completed. as long as the quality of social protection systems and in particular the pensions of one member state differ greatly from those of another member state of the european union, the real europe will not be completed. finally, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, until real convergence is achieved, until economic and social cohesion is a reality for all, until the european social model is developed, the real europe will not be completed. we do not expect the lisbon summit to resolve all these questions. we say this with the realism of those who know that social policy and its implementation remain the responsibility of the member states, and who wish that this should not change. in these circumstances, it is through coordination that we must proceed in such a way as to achieve the maximum common output of the various interdependent and organised institutional processes, in order to develop the social policies which the european union already has. however, and in conclusion, we hope that the lisbon summit will not be limited to a summit where the analysis and diagnosis of social problems prevail and where the conclusions will be without consequences. more than conclusions, more than words, the peoples of the member states of the european union are waiting for answers and demanding action. we expect the portuguese presidency to understand the political significance of this difference.

- (pt) mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr commissioner, the only really fundamental aspect of the idea of drawing up a charter of fundamental rights of the union is the attempt, under generous appearances, to bring about the constitutionalisation of the states, or even, more directly, a european constitution of a european state. that is why we are opposed to it. the content of the charter is not in question. we come from a country whose constitution contains one of the most extensive lists of existing fundamental rights. and we have come from a party founded on the struggle for democracy, which has always considered the uncompromising defence of human rights as a priority. but we oppose this charter because we see in it the idea of a kind of 'trojan horse' of federalism, a political trap which carries within it other political and legal consequences, which are heavy for the states and peoples of europe. we believe that at this stage it is not right, much less appropriate, to undermine or break the balance of the treaties. on the contrary, the times and the challenges are such that we believe it is more important than ever to defend the treaties and to re-engage on the safe paths traced by the founding countries that have brought us here. the european union does not need a charter of fundamental rights for the simplest of reasons: the european union has no fundamental rights problem. the europe of which we are a part is, in fact, to a large extent the cradle of the culture of fundamental rights. and since there is no problem, the charter is not a solution. and since it is not a solution to anything, the charter is a problem. firstly because it divides us on a subject that should unite us and secondly because it would create a superposition and perhaps a disturbing conflict with a european convention guardianship within the proven and stabilized institutional framework of the council of europe and the european court of human rights. it's not a plus, it's a minus. the proper seat for the enshrinement of fundamental rights is the constitutional law of each nation state. and the proper seat for international legal consultation and for real progress in the international protection of human rights are the pacts and conventions ratified by states in as wide a geographical area as possible. i am referring to the universal declaration of human rights and, in the european context, to the european convention. we must work within these broader international frameworks to make the protection of rights already recognised by states really more effective, instead of embarking on a path which would be reductive because it would run counter to the main pillar of the culture of fundamental rights: its universalism. if the european union is to achieve a truly emblematic work in this field, it must follow a different path: it must include in the catalogue of international reference texts, in article 6 of the treaty on european union, all the human rights pacts and conventions currently ratified by the 15 member states, in order to incorporate them into the community acquis. that would be a real breakthrough. this charter is a false answer to a non-existent question and therefore becomes an additional problem that we could do without.
- (pt) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the winds are blowing in the european union in the direction of the environment and the environment is being welcomed by the european parliament. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment against the adverse effects of ionizing radiation is a very good one. air pollution used to be the street; it was blending with the street. today, on a large scale, it's the entire planet, and on a small scale, it may even be our home. so, to summarize, this is our entire ecosystem. we have to do something. the setting of limit values for the four pollutants is a good step forward and will help to reduce pollution, combat acidification and the harmful effects of these pollutants on human health and vegetation. in its proposal for a directive, the european commission proposes quantified national ceilings to be reached by 2010 for the four pollutants in question. moreover, the costs that each member state will have to bear to achieve these values have been estimated. i think that's a good strategy. the report by mrs myller is excellent and it clearly considers that the european commission's proposal is balanced, while seeking to strengthen it in certain areas. the committee approved the compulsory revision of the directive by 2004, which will make it possible to review the limit values set by the text and the measures taken to comply with these limit values. all of this seems reasonable. but i have to ask myself a question here. why not accept that a member state can demonstrate that it is particularly disadvantaged in the implementation of the directive, for example because of its geographical location and climate, and that it suffers from serious disadvantages as a result? wouldn 't that be prudent ? the european union's system for combating air pollution is therefore being expanded, strengthened and refined. the environmental policy of the european union is increasingly becoming the environmental policy of each member state, with clear benefits for all european citizens.

in writing. - (pt) we reject at the outset the principles of the admissibility and generalization to all member states of the manufacture of chocolate with a percentage of vegetable fat other than cocoa butter. this is for reasons connected with the protection of the legitimate interests of the cocoa-producing countries and the defence of consumers. it is obvious that such a measure will inevitably have negative consequences for the cocoa-producing countries. our position is further motivated by the aggravating circumstance that no study has been carried out on the impact of this measure on developing cocoa-producing countries. the setting of a 5% limit for vegetable fats other than cocoa butter in chocolate manufacture is also a dangerous precedent in that it could eventually lead not only to a higher percentage but also open the door to the production of chocolate using other fats, including those obtained by genetic modification. in addition, a method of analysis is still needed to determine the actual amount of these fats in chocolate production, and a labelling requirement to clearly distinguish between real chocolate containing only cocoa butter and chocolate containing other fats.
in writing. - (pt) the adoption of this directive on waste incineration, which will apply from 2005, significantly improves the current framework for waste management in europe, by promoting such important criteria as public health and the defence of citizens' rights. if this directive were to enter into force immediately, which unfortunately will not be the case until 2005, it would be virtually impossible for the portuguese government to continue its co-incineration strategy. in any case, as a directive which imposes significant technical, legal and procedural restrictions on all incineration and co-incineration processes in europe, it shows that the strategy followed by portugal is deeply flawed. i can therefore only welcome the results achieved, but i regret that no further steps have been taken in the restrictions to be introduced on incineration and co-incineration processes, since this directive1 - sets more stringent emission limit values, particularly for carbon oxides and dust; 2 - parallels the limit values for incineration and co-incineration plants, ending their previous exceptional status; 3 - considers, without leaving any doubt, that incineration and co-incineration may be used only as a last resort, after all other technical possibilities have been exhausted; 4 - sets very strict conditions for the overall management of waste, the location and size of incineration and co-incineration plants, which must be met before any licence can be granted; 5 - increases the power of intervention of the competent authorities in the event of non-compliance with the limit values by installations; 6 - drastically reduces the possibility of waste from co-incineration and incineration being used, particularly in civil construction; 7 - introduces continuous sampling of dioxins as a mandatory requirement for incineration and co-incineration processes; 8 - increases the participation of citizens in the choice of the location of facilities; 9 - ensures that an annual environmental report is published indicating compliance with the limit values; 10 - considerably limits the existing derogations for cement kiln installations; 11 - sets, for the first time, a limit value for ammonia (nh3) emissions, given the acidification of the atmosphere due to this chemical component.
- (pt) madam president, ladies and gentlemen, once again we are devoting part of our time in the plenary to one of the most noble themes of our parliamentary work, that of the defence of human rights, whatever their expression, wherever they may be in the world. i would like to congratulate the presidency on its happy decision to have these four reports discussed at the same time. although we do not have enough time to give each report its due importance, the fact is that when we discuss human rights outside - in the rest of the world - we do not exclude a serious analysis of what needs to be done inside, in the european union. if it is true, fortunately, that in the eu we do not see the gross violations that concern us in other states, it is no less true that in our states too we must improve respect for these rights, economic and social rights, civil and political rights, in particular respect for privacy and protection of personal data, the rights of children, women, immigrant refugees. we have a long way to go in terms of police and prison violence, and in the fight against racism and xenophobia. as regards third countries, it is essential and urgent that the european union should have a common strategy drawn up by the council with the participation of parliament, a policy which must integrate all the policies of the european union in a coherent way, in particular that of development cooperation. it is not sensible, in the name of realpolitik, to be more demanding of one country than the other, to treat some countries more leniently than others, and to pursue policies of cooperation with countries which do not respect the fundamental rights of the human person, where the rule of law does not function and where serious irregularities go unpunished. if we continue to support politically and economically countries where this is happening, we are complicit in regimes which do not respect human rights and in relation to which we are entitled to ask whether community aid reaches the people whose development it is claimed to promote or whether it only feeds the vanity, opulence and secret accounts of some of their leaders.
- (pt) mr secretary of state, i think you have clarified the technical question of articles 6 and 7 precisely and perfectly. i don't see anything wrong with that. the fundamental point to be taken into account here is that we are faced with a europe which has made great progress in economic terms, in the single market, in the single currency and which has done little or nothing in the field of security and justice. we still have a security and justice framework that is almost entirely national, while the realities are clearly transnational. we are constantly confronted with this contradiction, particularly in relation to children's rights. in some cases, more than one country is involved and not only do the victims not receive notification from the state where the judgment is given, but they are not even able to obtain legal aid.

- (pt) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it seems to me very important that the european parliament should take part in the debate on how the european union envisages the possibilities offered by the information society - the subject is on the agenda of the lisbon summit. while i would point out that many would expect the lisbon summit to provide clearer answers in this area, i do not deny the importance of the subject or the need to define a european strategy for this sector. we all know that this is a sensitive and vital area for our future and that the union is lagging far behind the united states. i agree with the report presented by mrs read and i would like to emphasize, in the words of the president of the council, mr antónio guterres, the three main shortcomings in this sector. first, the pragmatic deficit. as the read report rightly points out, the commission document is full of general guidelines and of intentions which are certainly commendable, but it has weaknesses in terms of concrete measures and consequent action. i do not want to believe that the council's conclusions are limited to this register. in this area, as in others, what we need is not more statements and speeches, but measures and decisions to reduce the gap between us and the united states. secondly, the democratic deficit. there is also a democratic deficit here - which has admittedly been greater in the past - but there is a need to combat the divide between those who have access to the internet, new technologies and the benefits of the information society and those who are deprived of them, for economic reasons or because of a lack of information and training in technology. building on the education system and transport and telecommunications structures that make internet access cheaper, faster and more powerful is therefore a priority that must be at the forefront of our concerns. and finally, the investment deficit. as the report rightly points out, the answer to these problems cannot be left to the market alone. much can and must be done in the field of health, public administration, the relationship between the state and the citizen, all public services, if europe is to make the qualitative leap we all recognise it needs.

in writing. - (pt) the question of animal health deserves our full attention, particularly as regards bovine and porcine species. hence the importance of animal health policy in intra-community trade in animals of these species. this approach must also take into account the need to pay greater attention to the permanent eradication of diseases such as leukaemia, tuberculosis and brucellosis, and to adequate health procedures. but it is important not to forget the need to support producers properly, with particular attention to small farms and family farming, since, as in portugal, often the income of small farmers, already low by nature, is deeply affected by these diseases, for which the low level of compensation does not offer sufficient compensation. in future, therefore, there must be a policy which is able to do justice to the interests of consumers as well as producers, and particularly small farmers.
in writing. - (pt) our abstention from voting on this report on the charter of fundamental rights of the european union is primarily due to our belief that this charter could be part of a federalistic logic, which we do not approve of, and which could find clear and definitive expression in the adoption of a constitution for the european union, as the report recommends, of which the charter would be an important part. moreover, the fact that we recognize ourselves in many of the rights proposed for inclusion in the charter (which are also contained in the constitution of the portuguese republic) has led us not to vote against it, even though we believe that the set of rights concerned could and should unambiguously include the social field, which would be the case if the amendments formulated in this regard by the gue/ngl were approved.

- (pt) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the tragic images that have been brought to us from mozambique following a rare succession of extremely severe natural disasters are still etched in our memories and in our eyes; we also have in mind a reflection on the slowness of the response to a disaster of this magnitude. we regard the resolution which will be voted on today as the result of an emergency debate which took place immediately after the brussels mini-session. we agree with all the points made in it: the need to continue mobilizing relief funds for displaced people, the assessment of the effects of the whole crisis and the need to coordinate and combine efforts to prevent all the foreseeable health consequences, to restore agricultural land and rebuild the country. however, we must not be the only ones to bear the burden of being late. it is also worth asking what other countries on other continents are doing. what have india and pakistan done, which have significant resources and large communities in mozambique? what have the closest african countries done? angola, for example, has a powerful army that could have provided valuable aid, or zimbabwe, its near neighbour, or nigeria, which has an operational air force. this question must also be asked in the context of the forthcoming eu/africa summit, so that we can communicate better around a shared concern for solidarity. i should also like to say that we are sensitive to the question of cancelling the external debt, but we are more in tune with the text of the joint resolution. i must say that as a christian democrat i am sensitive to the calls made by the church in this jubilee year for the external debt of the third world to be dealt with in a concrete way. but for many african countries, there are several situations to distinguish. there are cases where the wealth accumulated by the leaders of these countries would be sufficient to repay the external debt and so there are delicate problems that need to be properly assessed. we know that this is not the case in mozambique, if only because of the poverty of the country. but we think that the resolution as it appears in the motion for a resolution is correct and i must say that we approve of the position adopted by the portuguese government which has decided to cancel immediately half of mozambique's external debt. - (pt) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are all still asking ourselves how this was possible. the fact is that international mobilization was late and initially insufficient, except for the help from neighbouring countries, especially south africa. it was not until march that a large international aid began to arrive. the first is that the international organizational structures needed to deal with such disasters are lacking, because the technical means exist, the armies and security forces of the countries, which are prepared for search and rescue operations, are equipped with them. the balance sheet of the disaster is not yet complete: we know that nearly two million people have been affected, we know that the current shortage of drinking water is tragic and we know that many antipersonnel mines - there are an estimated one and a half million in the country - have been moved to fields and communication lines that have already been cleared. it is now a matter of rebuilding a country, a country which was the victim of a colonial war waged by a government which, in portugal too, oppressed the portuguese people until the liberation of 25 april 1974; the european union is also committed to the development of the social and economic situation in mozambique, which has been torn apart by a bloody civil war until recently, but which has embarked on a path of economic, social and political development that is internationally recognized as an example. the main purpose of this is to bring comfort and hope to the people of mozambique. we must absorb the shock and revive the country's economy. the international mobilization is to be welcomed at this time and the president of mozambique himself expressed his gratitude for the amount of aid provided. the european union must play a leading role. on the one hand, it has the capacity to make an effective material contribution and, on the other hand, the paris club has just decided to suspend debt servicing before reaching an agreement on its total cancellation. in our view, the countries of the european union must act quickly in this direction. on the other hand, the european union, in cooperation with the local authorities and other countries and organizations, including the united nations, can undertake an important organizational effort to support the reconstruction of mozambique. we will work towards this, we will give our full support to the international conference for the reconstruction of mozambique which will be organized by canada. we will work to ensure that the international community is equipped to respond rapidly to situations of serious humanitarian disasters. we in the european parliament will ensure that the european union plays its proper role. - (pt) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the aim of information on the common agricultural policy must be to establish a communication strategy which is mainly aimed at the agricultural and rural stakeholders. now, in our view, the main criticism of this commission proposal is that it opens up the budget line to non-agricultural organizations and also reduces the budget allocations to four and a half million euros. this situation is further aggravated by the reserve of funds which the commission has set aside for itself, which, in addition to the specific measures, leaves the organisations with only three million euros, which means a clear reduction in resources. the proposal put forward by the rapporteur is a positive one, since it proposes that the commission should adjust the budgetary allocation to the considerable number of organizations entitled to participate in initiatives under this regulation. but there is another negative aspect to the commission's proposal, which relates to the co-financing limit of 75%, whereas the actions directly carried out by the commission are financed at 100%. on the other hand, we know that the proposal for a regulatory vademecum makes it even more difficult for organisations to access the budget line in question, especially for those with the least financial resources, i.e. for the companies that need the most support. that is why the proposal we are presenting is so important, because it complements the ideas contained in the report. with our proposal we want community co-financing to exceed 75% for organisations representing small farmers and family farmers in financial difficulty. and we hope it will be approved. as regards the proposal to reduce the budget by eur 1 million for the commission in order to increase the number of specific measures, we believe that this may be a possible solution to the commission's proposal for a regulation. finally, it is important to stress that the commission must simplify the administrative procedures, both in the examination of the programmes submitted by the organisations and for their subsequent control.

- (pt) madam president, commissioner, i congratulate the commission on this initiative, which i consider extremely important. but i would like the commissioner to explain how it is possible to take this initiative against the background of a common agricultural policy which is completely opposed to the defence of food safety and quality. if agricultural policy gives subsidies essentially to produce and is based solely on the quantitative criterion of production, if farmers receive money according to the quantities they produce, how can we accept such a directive without first adapting agricultural policy to a quality policy, to a food security policy? or is it simply a way of dealing with a public opinion that is increasingly wary of the food chain, but which cannot be changed by this type of agricultural policy? why not start by adapting the agricultural policy to give consumers full confidence?
- (pt) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by congratulating our colleague, mr hernández mollar, on the excellent work he has done and on the excellent report he is presenting to us. he stressed that the issues of immigration and asylum, although closely linked, are nevertheless of a different nature and therefore need to be addressed differently by the european union, although it is recalled and recognised that it is advantageous to adopt an inter-pillar and comprehensive approach in both cases. we know that more and more people are seeking better opportunities or protection by migrating to escape war, persecution, poverty, unemployment or human rights violations. we know that there is an increase in illegal migration movements, in most cases managed by criminal networks which frequently generate situations of exploitation and flagrant violations of human rights. we are clearly in favour of a comprehensive vision which will have to take into account support for democratisation and the promotion of the rule of law, conflict prevention and reconciliation, respect for human rights and the development, within countries and regions of origin and transit, of the fight against poverty with a view to improving living conditions and providing more employment opportunities. parliament must therefore take serious and substantive action, beyond the criticisms which mr hernández mollar has just made before this house, especially in terms of parliament's action and the effectiveness of that action in some of these countries. we cannot formulate good intentions and not put them into practice because of lack of financial means. information campaigns should be carried out in close cooperation with countries of origin and transit on the real possibilities of legal emigration, in order to try to reduce or address the source of the problem. it is necessary to strengthen the fight against illegal immigration, to ensure the protection of the rights of victims of criminal networks, to intensify efforts to prevent all forms of trafficking in human beings, paying particular attention to the situation of women's and children's rights, and to detect and dismantle criminal networks involved in these activities. we must carry out within the union a thorough assessment of the situation in the various member states and a convergence of national legislation on the conditions of admission and residence, taking into account the economic and demographic evolution of the union, the reception capacity of each member state and the historical and cultural links between each member state and the countries of origin. in summary, we advocate a common european policy on asylum and increasing immigration based on four pillars: a partnership with countries of origin, a common european asylum policy, fair treatment of people from third countries and effective management of migration flows.
- (pt) mr president, as we all know, the action plans currently being drawn up by the council's high level group are a precarious instrument for dealing with a problem that for some is only a security problem, and for others only an integration problem, so that it is almost impossible to know where the line between the right to asylum and the right to economic survival lies most of the time. and i am speaking of a precarious instrument because the analyses on which the action plans are based do not seem to me to provide any useful new elements that justify effective policy measures, because most of the time they are generic proposals for conventional foreign policy or development aid that would only achieve their goal in the eyes of the authorities of the target countries that are legitimate and stable, which is unfortunately rarely the case. hence the temptation, in these action plans and in the measures proposed, to put more emphasis on the management of illegal immigration, on repression and control, than on the integration of citizens, on the establishment of a european asylum policy and a real system of temporary protection for those who knock at the door of europe for whatever reason, always in a real state of need. the action plans which tampere welcomed and called for to be continued, despite their fragility in the face of the complexity of the problem, can meanwhile be improved while waiting for the european union to have its own asylum and immigration policy. however, they cannot strike a balance between the relative uselessness and the worsening human rights situation, whereas as asylum seekers, immigrants cannot be considered potential criminals, and would be better treated normally as cases of universal exclusion rather than as 'police cases'. government experts. we do not, however, lose hope that we will soon be able to discuss these issues in a dialogue with commissioner antónio vitorino, as a sign that europe has made progress in the field of common policies based on a balance between security and integration and that it has firmly opted for the end of precariousness.

- the president of the council, mr commissioner, at the end of these interventions it has been established that there is an organized system controlled by the american secret services which is able to intercept almost all forms of voice and data communications on a global scale. what is not yet a fact, but what we fear and suspect, is that the system has been reoriented not to pursue purely military and defence objectives, but commercial, industrial and technological ones. we must therefore ask three questions. the first question, addressed to the council and the commission, is: does the term fair trade make sense if large american companies can benefit from illegitimate advantages through the privileged information that this system provides them? secondly, what is the point of civil liberties, a principle we hold so dear, if the privacy of our heads of state, our governments and our citizens can be violated without any crime prevention and without a judicial mandate, as our laws require? what would the council and the commission think of the reaction of the american state if it learned that europe was capable of intercepting the communications of its president, its governments and its citizens? so how do we defend ourselves against this threat? the commissioner has already spelled out the efforts in the area of cryptography, which was already mentioned in the last european parliament recommendation. but what has been done to strengthen the cryptographic system, to invest in european technology to ensure that we are better protected against this threat? finally, what is the council's and the commission's view of the recent french decision to eliminate from its public systems commercial computer operating systems whose source code is closed, thus allowing the adoption of open source systems, and this - as the french minister of defence says - in order to ensure that no nation or no defence system is at the mercy of one man, at the mercy of one company?

- (pt) madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, over the last decade the need for a policy in favour of children has matured and has been given concrete expression by the 1989 united nations convention on the rights of the child, which has been ratified by all the member states. since then, attention to this issue has increased considerably in all member states. however, at the eu level - although children represent almost one fifth of the population of the union - very little practical attention is paid to their needs in economic policies, as well as in other areas. thousands of children worldwide are victims of serious and systematic violations of their human rights, ranging from undetected and unpunished domestic cruelty and the economic exploitation of their labour force, depriving them of the right to education and a childhood, to their use in war; the violations range from the horrifying depiction of child soldiers to their use for prostitution or pornography. the economic and social committee estimates that more than 5 million children are working in europe. many of these children work in such areas as pornography, drug trafficking, child pornography, and sex tourism. these children especially need the protection of society. the evolution of the internet, its rapid development and its growing importance raise the question of the use of children for criminal purposes. at the previous session here in strasbourg we discussed the use we should make of the internet: the union's desire is to move faster in terms of e-commerce and to invest more decisively in new technologies and education. we are debating today what we must avoid and what we must fight against: the circulation and dissemination of child pornography, which has increased in a frightening way. and while we certainly need to encourage not only the administrators of websites and service providers to set their own guidelines and regulations, but also internet users to inform the authorities whenever they come across child pornography material, state and union law has a responsibility to address this problem. the excellent report by our colleague mr kirkhope clearly shows what we still need to do and improve in the area of setting up special units, in strengthening cooperation and exchanges between states, in action at europol level, in the effectiveness of action by member states and even, where necessary, in the revision of their own legislation and processes in this area, so as to ensure the application of effective criminal sanctions. as regards the responsibility of the union as such, i welcome the fact that the charter of fundamental rights, which is being drafted, envisages the specific rights of children and takes into account the areas in which the union can give a strong impetus, more specifically in these areas.
- (pt) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, many of us have repeatedly called for the creation of a european refugee fund. it was not until the striking examples of kosovo - and to a lesser extent timor - that the need for this fund became clearer. the entry into force of the treaty of amsterdam has opened up a new perspective for european asylum and immigration policy, which, as our colleague mr oostlander has already pointed out, presupposes solidarity between the member states, and that is why mechanisms must be created to help achieve a balance between the efforts made by the member states which take in these refugees and those which can bear the consequences of this reception in the short and long term. it seems to us justified that this fund should contain an emergency component to be applied in cases of massive flows of persons in need of international protection, but which at the same time guarantees the financing of structural policy measures, i.e. which allows, in a multiannual programming framework, to provide effective support for measures aimed firstly at improving reception conditions, secondly at promoting integration, thirdly at facilitating voluntary repatriation and finally at facilitating reintegration. the kosovo experience has shown that a large proportion of displaced persons, even those who have been granted refugee status, are willing to return to their country of origin if the necessary conditions are met. i would therefore like to emphasize what our colleague mr pirker has already said about the difference between immigrants and temporary refugees. while in the first case, policies to support integration need to be developed, in the second case, policies to support the return of refugees to the country or region they had to flee should be put in place in the interests of the refugees. these two situations are different and so are the responses to them. to confuse them is to give in to demagogy. i would also like to stress the need to speed up the creation of legal instruments to make available the funds already earmarked for this purpose in the 2000 budget. it would also be necessary to lay down specific rules on the criteria and arrangements to be applied for the allocation of these funds and the operation of the respective control mechanisms.
the rapporteur. - (pt) mr president, the treaty of amsterdam has considerably strengthened the system for protecting the financial interests of the european union by reviewing and deepening the mechanisms created by the maastricht treaty with the new article 280. thus, beyond the principle introduced at maastricht, according to which member states are obliged to take measures similar to those taken to combat fraud affecting their financial interests, amsterdam adds two new elements: the european parliament now has a codecision power; the protection of the community's financial interests must be effective and equivalent. the entry into force of the treaty of amsterdam therefore entails the application of the principle of equality of all citizens and the equivalence of all acts before the law in the field of the protection of financial interests. mr president, this is the first piece of legislation on the prevention of fraud and other irregularities since the entry into force of the treaty of amsterdam. the amendment is intended to prevent fraud and other irregularities in the common agricultural policy. the cap accounted for up to 87% of community expenditure in 1970, its weight has slowly fallen over the last thirty years to about 45% of community expenditure at present. it is therefore understandable that legislation on the prevention and fight against fraud is developed independently in the context of the cap and not by means of general rules applicable to the whole budget. until a few years ago, the fight against agricultural fraud was the responsibility of the directorate-general for agriculture rather than the appropriate department, uclaf. today, with the amsterdam treaty in force, this is no longer acceptable. we cannot allow the protection of the community's financial interests to continue to be based on separate, inconsistent and contradictory legislation, which treats the various infringements of the community's financial interests by virtue of the concrete expenditure to which it refers and not by virtue of the principles of equity. in the field of the protection of financial interests, we must put an end to the closure of the various chapters of community expenditure and ensure that there is a single weight and measure in each of them. the reform of the commission can no longer be a meaningless old debate and a metaphysical discussion of the advantages and disadvantages of different models of ex ante and ex post control, of decentralisation. this reform must be understood as a determination to put an end to the feudal fiefs and bureaucratic chapels. that is why we are proposing to the house that the legal basis for the regulation on the prevention of fraud and irregularities be changed from article 37 to article 280. just as the commission and the european institutions have come to accept and understand that the fight against fraud must be carried out effectively and equally in all its actions, by giving this competence to olaf, so it is now for the commission to accept that the legislation for the prevention of fraud and irregularities should also be developed effectively and equally in strict compliance with the treaty of amsterdam. we are confident that the commission will eventually agree fully to this principle.
- (pt) madam president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to point out some positive conclusions of the lisbon european council, focusing in particular on the draft joint resolution signed by my group and others and so much criticised by the socialists. these conclusions were made by the heads of state or government as part of a strategic objective to make the european union the most competitive area in the world within the next ten years, through the development of a knowledge-based economy and the adoption of policies for growth and full employment. question: do the statements made by the present european leaders, most of whom are socialists and have been in business for several years, not reveal above all their own incapacity and the serious flaws in the european model of development which they have promoted? what is there really new and original in the conclusions of the lisbon summit that was not diagnosed and affirmed in the 1993 white paper on growth, competitiveness and employment? the emphasis was then on 'information networks', but now it is on 'the field of knowledge and access to the information society'. the emphasis was on "lifelong learning" and now it is on "lifelong learning". and we could go on with countless conclusions which are exactly the same as those in the 1993 white paper. one aspect, however, deserves special mention in the field of full employment policies. the objective was to halve the number of unemployed in the european union by 1993 from 17 million to 8.5 million. in fact, 15 million people are unemployed in the year 2000 and the summit conclusions promise us rates close to full employment for the coming decade. it is at least politically irresponsible and reveals a mentality peculiar to socialists, even to the adherents of the third way, based on the voluntarist development of a strategy which everyone knows is the product of multiple factors and which is ultimately centred on the commitment of enterprises. as one well-known analyst put it, european leaders have decided something they cannot afford to do. to set ambitious targets for growth and employment, as they have done, is to decree an increase in the population or the number of days of sunshine. we believe that governments must first focus on developing the means for social and economic actors to generate wealth and to show positive results in these areas. another aspect which deserves our attention is the fact that the summit apparently ignored the fact that the countries of the european union have different historical backgrounds and different economic and social structures, that the disparities will be accentuated by enlargement to the east and that there are still asymmetries which even the presentation of average rates of development cannot hide. this applies to both the positive and the negative aspects. how can we explain, for example, that sweden and finland, countries with highly developed social protection models, have internet penetration rates identical to those in the united states, about 50%, while the rate is 29% in england, which is much more liberal? how is it possible that the union still has glaring disparities in minimum wages, or significant differences in the quality of social security systems and the amount of pensions, or in unemployment or underemployment rates, not to mention ever-widening pockets of poverty and exclusion? the european union may have already begun to catch up with north america in terms of economic development, but the real europe has yet to be built because convergence of national economies and internal economic and social cohesion have not been its first priority.
- (pt) madam president, i would like to greet the president of the council and wish him every success in the exercise of the portuguese presidency of the european union. we all broadly agree with the strategic objective of the lisbon summit to make the european union 'the most dynamic and competitive economic area in the world, based on knowledge and capable of ensuring sustainable economic growth'. no one will doubt the importance of preparing our citizens for the demands of the information society, knowledge and new communication technologies so that they have the strategic tools for future success, by creating jobs and higher levels of quality and pay. there would be no problem if the lisbon summit had been convened specifically to discuss these kinds of problems, if it had been a summit on the information society, a summit on the knowledge economy, a summit on the new economy, depending on tastes, sensitivities or pretensions. but to call this summit the employment summit is to divert it from its objective. it's like putting a false label on a product, a label that doesn't match the contents of the packaging. in this case, political expediency. the aim of employment is, in fact, far more than these questions. first, because the new economy cannot dynamite the old economy, since this is essentially a generational challenge. the old economy supports the reality that is ours and whose modernization and structural adjustment are an indispensable step. this implies that we should already be discussing strategies for the conversion of the traditional sectors of our economy, as would be required by consideration of the options we will have to take in terms of the guiding principles of the world trade organisation. secondly, because a strategic debate on employment should begin with a substantial analysis of the factors of competitiveness in the european union, because it is on this plane that the sustainable growth of the economy and, therefore, of employment and social welfare, is at stake. in fact, in a debate of this kind, we cannot help but wonder why the european union has had a growth rate lower than that of the united states of america in recent years or why no government has had the courage to question the so-called 'european social model' in its most negative aspects because, without questioning its positive aspects, this model in some respects invites passivity, ease, and indolence for sections of the population who could and should work. we should therefore ask ourselves why the rates of economic reintegration of the working population benefiting from solidarity policies have been so low, as in the particular case of systems such as the 'guaranteed minimum income' or why europe has such a static and interventionist tradition, which negatively affects the competitiveness of the economy and why, under the guise of social and stability arguments, it sometimes conceals strategies of spending and even, often, of managing political clienteles. these have a negative impact on economic growth, the distribution of wealth and, consequently, on employment. a thorough debate on the employment problem in the european union cannot therefore ignore these questions.
- (pt) mr president, the results of the lisbon summit show that everything that the portuguese presidency had presented as a priority for employment and the fight against social exclusion was nothing but a big hoax. the supporters of this summit may use pink ribbons to wrap up the package of conclusions, whether they speak of the so-called modernisation of the european social model or of the need to make the european union the most competitive area in the world, but the fact remains that the objectives are to free the labour market from the conditions imposed by legislation protecting workers' rights, to place social security at the mercy of the financial markets, to accelerate the process of liberalisation of fundamental sectors such as gas, electricity, postal services, transport and telecommunications. so instead of quality employment with rights, reduced working hours with wage maintenance and no greater flexibility, the decisions of this summit will in practice contribute to a lower quality, more precarious employment with fewer rights and no effective social protection. instead of high-quality public services that respond to users' needs, protect the most vulnerable sections of the population and create quality jobs, the council's decisions are geared towards accelerating privatisation to satisfy the interests of economic groups and increase their profits, with negative consequences for employment, working conditions, the environment and land use planning. the only positive thing in lisbon was the large demonstration of tens of thousands of workers marching to denounce the increasing precariousness of employment, unemployment and social exclusion, to demand more quality jobs, better wages, more social rights and changes in the direction of european policies, in order to achieve economic and social cohesion.
- (pt) mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr president of the commission, we had the opportunity in january and last month to congratulate the portuguese presidency on its initiative in organising this extraordinary council and on the depth and seriousness of its preparation. we would like to add to this, after the summit, our congratulations on the conclusions of this council and to congratulate president romano prodi on the way in which the commission, under his presidency, contributed to the document transmitted in time for the council's work. but, mr president, like all conclusions, these have a defect: they do not change reality overnight. we are witnessing today many speeches by members who would have liked to see the portuguese presidency come here and say: 'we had 15 million unemployed before the summit and we have only 10 million today; - (pt) mr president, i should like to say first of all that the fact that this summit has taken place is a positive thing in itself. we have always felt that every effort should be made towards this objective, because we have always thought that this would be a good opportunity to launch a genuine partnership of mutual benefit for both continents. but we also think that the results of this summit are far from what was possible and desirable. i think that the difficulties of the journey may have led to the idea that the main thing was only that this event took place, that there was a formal meeting and a photo of the occasion, and not what could and should come out of it in order to put into practice the new basis of a strategy for this partnership. i do not think that the conclusions are up to the needs and possibilities of the moment. it is clear that economic issues are a prerequisite for a genuine rapprochement between the two continents and the individual positions of certain member states, although limited in scope and positive in nature, cannot make this forgotten. i am referring in particular to the cancellation of the external debt of the developing countries. the european union, which has systematically and wrongly retreated in cooperation, is conditioned or determined in cooperation with africa by two fundamental conceptions: on the one hand, by the prospect of transferring traditional and preferential relations within the restricted framework of the wto (this was evident during the negotiations on the agreement to replace the lomé convention); the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is clear that with these prospects and in the face of the enormous problems and needs of african countries in general in the fields of economy, environment, health, infrastructure, conflict resolution, poverty, among others, it is difficult, if not impossible, to establish a genuine effective and sustainable partnership. this is the direction we must take in the future. i say 'we must' because, contrary to what has happened today, i hope that in the future the european parliament and the ngos will be involved in this process and also because i hope that this summit can contribute, at least, to greater awareness of the real problems in the field of cooperation with africa and that it can represent a call for a change of course in this field.

in writing. - (pt) it is good that the council's common position already partially incorporates the amendments voted by the european parliament. but it is necessary to insist, as the rapporteur proposes, on the inclusion of proposals approved at first reading and rejected by the council. following the adoption of the montreal protocol on biosafety in january 2000, further steps are needed to protect consumers' rights and restore their confidence. we therefore support the proposals to include in the directive a ban on the release of gmos containing antibiotic resistance marker genes, except for research purposes, and the requirement by the commission and the member states that no gmos and/or derived products leave the eu territory without the prior information and consent of the importing country. we are also in favour of total environmental responsibility, since it is unacceptable not to oblige biotechnology companies to take the necessary measures today to protect human health and reduce environmental risks. finally, in so far as member states and the commission allow deliberate release, they must ensure that measures are taken to prevent transfers of gmos' genes to other organisms in the environment.
in writing. - (pt) a system of identification of bovine animals and labelling of beef products must be set up and this system must come into force as soon as possible, which is not expected to take place before next september, given the co-decision process that is foreseen. the system will enable consumers to know the origin of the meat they are buying and to follow the history of the animal. this increased link between producer and consumer will allow potential risks to public health to be detected earlier and prevented more effectively. for beef imported from third countries, it is also necessary to apply identical labelling rules. if all the information required by community rules is not available, it is essential to affix a specific indication to the product clearly indicating 'non-community origin'. in order to protect consumers, the european parliament's report also proposes that operators and organisations producing and marketing minced beef, or cut beef or beef cuts should also be subject to the mandatory labelling scheme and not benefit from the derogations proposed by the commission, as this would be equivalent to exempting between 30% and 50% of beef from any labelling.
in writing. - (pt) the lisbon summit has created enormous expectations. some saw it as the 'employment summit', others as the 'innovation and knowledge society summit'. in the end, it was mostly the "internet summit", since that is where some of the most promising promises are concentrated - sorry for the pleonasm. i hope they come true. we agree with the expectations, but we would also like to be able to congratulate ourselves on the practical results. but only the future will allow us to do so. but we may regret certain frailties even now. the european leaders, most of them socialists, have not resisted the charming rhetoric of promising results that are not so much in their hands, without committing themselves to what is in fact in their hands: the means. we would have liked more practical attention to be given to two key points: reducing the tax burden and labour contributions; and effective deregulation and de-bureaucratisation, which stimulate entrepreneurial initiative and promote growth. the will to defend the 'european social model' is certain, but this defence cannot take place if it hinders economic dynamism and, ultimately, the very reality of the future social model and its quality. that is the meaning of our votes and the amendments tabled.
